~~~text
# MASTER PROMPT — AFET UYANIŞI
## Earthquake Chapter: 120-Second Emergency Awareness Challenge
### Unity 2D Isometric Pixel Art — Complete Gameplay Implementation

You are a *Senior Unity Gameplay Engineer, Technical Game Designer, Systems Architect, Narrative Designer, and Game Jam Technical Lead*.

Your mission is to inspect the existing Unity project named *AFET UYANIŞI* and implement a fully playable, visually polished, educational earthquake gameplay sequence.

Do not stop at planning, documentation, pseudocode, or isolated scripts. Implement the actual gameplay using the existing project structure, scenes, characters, sprites, animations, prefabs, and UI.

*IMPORTANT: This is a 2D ISOMETRIC PIXEL ART GAME, NOT A SIDE-SCROLLING GAME.*

The project is being developed by four developers for a 48-hour game jam.

Preserve all working systems and coordinate changes to shared files.

---

# 1. CORE GAMEPLAY CONCEPT

The earthquake chapter begins with the main character, Arda, waking up in his bedroom.

Suddenly, his phone rings.

A cinematic phone notification animation appears.

The phone displays:

*"AFET FARKINDALIK SİMÜLASYONU"*

*"DEPREM SENARYOSU: 02:00"*

*"Çevreni incele, riskleri belirle ve güvenli davranışları seç!"*

This is a fictional educational countdown. It must never imply that real earthquakes can reliably be predicted two minutes in advance.

When the player dismisses the notification, a 120-second gameplay timer begins.

The player must explore the house, interact with objects, identify hazards, answer emergency-awareness questions, and make appropriate safety decisions before the simulation timer expires.

The game combines:

- Isometric exploration.
- Interactive furniture and household objects.
- Risk recognition.
- Timed dialogue choices.
- Time penalties.
- Environmental consequences.
- Cinematic earthquake effects.
- Educational feedback.
- Performance-based results.

The main objective is not simply to click correct answers.

The player should *learn to recognize dangerous environments and make safer decisions through gameplay*.

---

# 2. PLAYER EXPERIENCE

The gameplay should feel like a small polished indie adventure, not a multiple-choice exam.

The intended emotional progression is:

1. Peaceful morning.
2. Sudden phone interruption.
3. Growing urgency.
4. Exploration and environmental discovery.
5. Important decisions under time pressure.
6. Earthquake protection sequence.
7. Consequences and educational feedback.

Target gameplay duration: approximately 3–5 minutes, including introduction, challenge, earthquake sequence, and results.

---

# 3. INTRODUCTION CINEMATIC

The boy begins asleep in his bed.

Use the existing sleeping and waking character assets whenever available.

Sequence:

1. The room is peaceful.
2. The clock displays 07:00.
3. The boy is sleeping.
4. A phone ringtone interrupts the ambience.
5. The phone vibrates on the bedside table.
6. The boy wakes up.
7. A short wake-up animation plays.
8. The camera subtly focuses on the phone.
9. The phone notification interface slides into view.
10. The player sees the simulation announcement.
11. The player presses "BAŞLA".
12. The 120-second countdown begins.

The phone animation should have:

- Smooth pixel-perfect movement.
- Phone vibration.
- Notification sound.
- Darkened background.
- Readable Turkish typography.
- Minimal UI clutter.
- Keyboard and mouse support.

The player must be able to dismiss the phone notification.

Do not create unnecessarily long unskippable cinematics.

---

# 4. MAIN GAMEPLAY SYSTEM — 120-SECOND COUNTDOWN

Create a reusable EmergencyCountdownManager.

Starting duration:

*120 seconds*

Display the countdown in the top-right corner.

Example:

01:59

The timer decreases during active gameplay.

It should pause during cinematics, loading screens, and noninteractive transitions.

Normal interaction dialogue should be brief and should not unintentionally consume time while the player is unable to act.

Support:

- StartTimer()
- PauseTimer()
- ResumeTimer()
- ApplyTimePenalty(seconds)
- AddTimeBonus(seconds), only if explicitly used by the game design
- GetRemainingTime()
- OnTimerExpired

The timer must never become negative.

All penalties should be configurable through the Unity Inspector.

### Timer appearance

120–61 seconds:

Normal white countdown.

60–31 seconds:

Amber countdown.

30–11 seconds:

Red countdown.

10–0 seconds:

Subtle pulsing animation and an audible ticking effect.

Avoid excessive flashing or uncomfortable screen effects.

---

# 5. INTERACTIVE HOUSEHOLD OBJECT SYSTEM

Create a reusable interaction system for objects in the isometric house.

The player moves using WASD or arrow keys.

Press E to interact with nearby objects.

Each interactable object should have:

- Unique identifier.
- Display name.
- Interaction radius.
- Optional animation.
- Educational explanation.
- Hazard classification.
- Configurable time penalty.
- Completion state.
- Optional gameplay event.
- Visual feedback.

Use an interface such as IInteractable or an equivalent modular architecture.

Do not hardcode all object behavior into the player controller.

## Example object interactions

### A. Mirror — Hazardous proximity

The player approaches a large wall mirror.

If the player remains inside the mirror's marked danger zone for a configurable duration, apply a penalty.

Default:

*-10 seconds*

Show:

"CAM TEHLİKESİ!"

"Deprem sırasında cam ve kırılabilecek eşyalardan uzak dur."

Do not penalize merely looking at or inspecting the mirror.

Penalize prolonged unnecessary exposure to the hazard zone.

The player must have a reasonable opportunity to recognize and leave the zone.

The warning should trigger before the penalty.

Avoid applying the penalty every frame.

Use a one-time penalty or an explicitly configured cooldown.

### B. Bookshelf — Falling-object hazard

The player approaches an unsecured bookshelf.

The player can inspect it.

Show a visual warning about unsecured tall furniture.

If the game supports a pre-earthquake preparation interaction, allow the player to identify or secure it through a short contextual action.

Do not suggest attempting to secure heavy furniture during actual shaking.

### C. Window — Glass hazard

The player approaches the window.

Display an environmental clue showing that glass may break.

If the player remains unnecessarily close during the earthquake sequence, show a danger consequence.

### D. Sturdy table — Protective location

The player examines a sturdy table.

The interaction teaches the player that the table may provide suitable cover in the depicted room.

During the earthquake, the player can perform:

*ÇÖK — KAPAN — TUTUN*

Use an animation showing the character taking cover under the sturdy table and holding on.

### E. Emergency bag

The player can inspect an emergency bag.

Show a short educational explanation of its purpose.

Do not encourage the player to search for or retrieve the bag during active shaking.

### F. Door and stairs

The player can inspect the exit route.

Teach the player that rushing to stairs or outside during shaking is unsafe.

After shaking stops, allow the player to assess hazards and follow a safe evacuation route if appropriate.

### G. Heavy furniture

Show potentially unstable furniture as a recognizable environmental risk.

The player should learn to avoid unsafe proximity during shaking.

---

# 6. TIMED MOTHER DIALOGUE SYSTEM

Create a reusable TimedDecisionController.

At specific narrative moments, the mother asks the player an earthquake-awareness question.

The game presents multiple-choice options.

The player has:

*5 seconds*

to select an answer.

The UI should display:

- Mother's portrait.
- Character name: ANNE.
- Question.
- Three or four answers.
- Five-second visual timer.
- Selected answer feedback.

### Penalty rules

Correct answer:

- No time penalty.
- Short positive feedback.
- Continue gameplay.

Incorrect answer:

- Apply a configurable penalty.
- Default: 30 seconds.
- Display a short explanation.

No answer within five seconds:

- Apply a 30-second penalty.
- Display "SÜRE DOLDU!"
- Show the correct safety explanation.

The penalty reduces the remaining simulation preparation time.

It does not change the real-world earthquake's physical speed.

### Question 1

Anne:

"Deprem sırasında ne yapmalıyız?"

Answers:

A. Pencereye koşmalıyız.

B. Sağlam bir masanın altında Çök–Kapan–Tutun yapmalıyız.

C. Asansöre binmeliyiz.

D. Merdivene koşmalıyız.

Correct answer:

B.

### Question 2

Anne:

"Deprem sırasında asansörü kullanmak doğru mu?"

Answers:

A. Evet, daha hızlı ineriz.

B. Hayır, asansörden uzak durmalıyız.

C. Sadece elektrik varsa kullanabiliriz.

Correct answer:

B.

### Question 3

Anne:

"Deprem sırasında camlara yakın durmalı mıyız?"

Answers:

A. Evet.

B. Hayır, camlardan uzak durmalıyız.

C. Pencereden dışarı bakmalıyız.

Correct answer:

B.

### Question 4

Anne:

"Sarsıntı bittikten sonra ilk ne yapmalıyız?"

Answers:

A. Çevremizdeki tehlikeleri kontrol etmeliyiz.

B. Hemen asansöre binmeliyiz.

C. Hasarlı binaya geri dönmeliyiz.

Correct answer:

A.

Questions must be stored in configurable data assets.

Use ScriptableObjects or another lightweight data-driven solution.

Do not embed all dialogue content inside one monolithic script.

---

# 7. DYNAMIC PENALTY SYSTEM

Create a TimePenaltySystem.

Examples:

| Player action | Penalty |
|---|---|
| Correct question answer | 0 seconds |
| Incorrect answer | 30 seconds |
| No answer within five seconds | 30 seconds |
| Prolonged mirror hazard exposure | 10 seconds |
| Ignoring a clearly marked environmental warning | Configurable |
| Correctly inspecting a hazard | 0 seconds |

Penalties should be meaningful but fair.

Do not stack multiple penalties from the same event unintentionally.

Do not penalize the player for merely exploring an educational object.

Show visual feedback when a penalty occurs:

-10 SANİYE

or

-30 SANİYE

Animate the countdown changing to its new value.

Play a short sound effect.

Do not use excessively harsh punishment or misleading educational explanations.

---

# 8. EARTHQUAKE EVENT

When the 120-second simulation timer reaches zero, trigger the earthquake event.

The player should experience a short cinematic earthquake sequence.

Required effects:

- Camera shake.
- Low-frequency rumbling audio.
- Furniture shaking.
- Lamp swinging.
- Books falling.
- Dust particles.
- Optional brief lighting changes.
- Character startled reaction.

Use scripted animations instead of expensive full physics simulations.

The player must then perform an appropriate protective action.

*Important:* The actual shaking is a separate gameplay state. The 120-second preparation timer is finished and must not continue counting down during the shaking.

If the player is near a suitable sturdy table, enable the Çök–Kapan–Tutun interaction.

If the player is elsewhere, provide an appropriate protection action based on the environment without forcing the player to run across a dangerous room during shaking.

Do not reward running toward the table after violent shaking has already begun.

Do not depict doorways, elevators, windows, or stairs as universally safe earthquake protection locations.

---

# 9. PROTECTION MINI-GAME

Create a short earthquake protection interaction.

The player must:

1. Drop to a protective position.
2. Cover head and neck.
3. Hold on during shaking.

The interaction should be easy to understand.

Possible input:

Hold E to maintain the protective position.

Duration:

Approximately 5–8 seconds.

The character animation should visibly communicate correct protective behavior.

Do not require rapid button mashing.

Incorrect or delayed input should produce clear educational feedback.

Avoid gratuitous injury animations.

---

# 10. POST-EARTHQUAKE SEQUENCE

After the shaking stops:

- Stop camera shake.
- Settle furniture animations.
- Display environmental changes.
- Show fallen objects and possible glass hazards.
- Allow the player to assess surroundings.
- Enable appropriate exit interactions.
- Prevent elevator use.
- Guide the player toward a safe assembly area if the route is appropriate.

Keep this sequence short.

The educational goal is to reinforce safe post-earthquake behavior.

---

# 11. RESULT AND PERFORMANCE SYSTEM

At the end of the chapter, show a results screen.

Example:

*AFET FARKINDALIK RAPORU*

Display:

- Correct decisions.
- Incorrect decisions.
- Environmental hazards identified.
- Unnecessary hazard exposure.
- Protection action completed.
- Remaining preparation time before the earthquake.

Possible performance labels:

- AFET BİLİNCİ USTASI
- HAZIRLIKLI
- GELİŞİYOR
- DAHA FAZLA ÖĞREN

Do not use only remaining time to determine the final result.

Reward correct safety decisions and hazard recognition.

Time pressure should support learning, not overshadow it.

Include short explanations for incorrect decisions.

---

This is a 2D isometric game.

Use the existing isometric Grid and Tilemap setup.

Requirements:

- Orthographic camera.
- Isometric player movement.
- Eight-directional character animations when available.
- Correct collision.
- Y-based sprite sorting or the project's existing equivalent.
- Pixel-perfect rendering.
- Point filtering for pixel art.
- Consistent character scale.
- Proper interaction ranges.
- No unnecessary platformer physics.

Preserve the existing main character's visual identity.

Do not replace him with a generic placeholder unless an asset is genuinely unavailable.

Reuse the existing sleeping and waking animation assets where suitable.

11. A timeout applies a 30-second penalty.
12. Correct answers do not apply penalties.
13. Penalties cannot accidentally repeat every frame.
14. The earthquake begins when the simulation timer expires.
15. The player can perform a protective action.
16. The shaking stops and the aftermath state begins.
17. The results screen displays actual player performance.
18. Existing art assets and teammate work remain intact.
19. Gameplay runs smoothly on an ordinary Windows laptop.
20. The full gameplay loop can be completed without manual Unity Editor intervention after setup.

---

# 16. IMPLEMENTATION INSTRUCTIONS

First inspect the existing project.

Identify:

- Unity version.
- Current scenes.
- Main character controller.
- Existing dialogue system.
- Existing interaction system.
- Existing animation assets.
- Existing UI.
- Existing earthquake effects.
- Git status.
- Teammate ownership rules.

Then implement the smallest complete vertical slice.

Do not rewrite functioning systems unnecessarily.

Do not overwrite teammates' work.

Create prefabs, components, animations, scene references, and UI—not just scripts.

Compile and test where Unity Editor access is available.

If Unity Editor access is unavailable, create valid Unity-compatible assets and clearly identify what still requires Editor testing.

At the end, report:

- Files created.
- Files modified.
- Mechanics implemented.
- Scene changes.
- Tests performed.
- Remaining issues.
- Manual setup requirements.

*FINAL OBJECTIVE:*

Deliver a polished, playable, 2D isometric earthquake-awareness chapter where the player wakes up, receives a phone alert, explores the house under a 120-second simulation countdown, interacts with environmental hazards, answers five-second questions from his mother, receives time penalties for unsafe decisions, experiences an earthquake, performs protective actions, and receives educational feedback.

*BUILD THE GAMEPLAY. DO NOT STOP AT PLANNING.*
~~~

~~~text
You are a senior Unity 2D game feel and technical animation developer.

Improve the game feel of our 2D isometric pixel-art flood disaster mini-game.

The player explores a neighborhood, receives official flood warnings, marks potential flood-risk areas on a map, and observes how rising water changes the environment.

Implement the following polish systems:

1. Dynamic rainfall intensity with lightweight pixel-art particles.
2. Three-stage flood progression using animated Tilemap overlays instead of expensive fluid simulation.
3. Water ripples, small splash effects, and floating environmental details.
4. Smooth camera transitions and subtle camera impulses for important events.
5. Animated smartphone notifications with sound and visual feedback.
6. Risk-map marker placement with pulse, highlight, and confirmation effects.
7. Dynamic ambient audio transitioning from light rain to severe storm.
8. Environmental reactions such as darkening lighting, water reflections, and occasional lightning.
9. A cinematic before-and-after reveal showing the consequences of player decisions.

Technical requirements:
- Unity 2D, C#, URP-compatible where applicable.
- Maintain crisp pixel-art rendering.
- Optimize for a 48-hour game jam.
- Avoid expensive real-time fluid simulation.
- Use modular scripts and configurable parameters.
- Respect player control and avoid excessive camera shake.
- Keep all critical flood safety information readable.

Prioritize responsiveness, clarity, atmosphere, and player feedback over graphical complexity.
~~~

## WP-015 — ROLE

- Kaynak mesaj: 189; satır: 3349; tarih/saat: 10/17/26 12:57:12 PM.
- Tür: Prompt / teknik istek; yapıldığı veya hangi araca gönderildiği tek başına kanıtlanmaz.
- Asıl mesaj gövdesi SHA256: `eafbb2096b60c1703576662530c1934ed632b70d48b91342eeee5f1f379ce9a2`.

~~~text
# ROLE

You are a senior Unity 2D gameplay engineer, technical game designer, UI/UX developer, and pixel-art animation specialist.

Your task is to implement a complete, polished, playable *Bookshelf Anchoring Mini-Game* inside our existing Unity project, *AFET UYANIŞI*.

This is a 48-hour game jam project. Prioritize a working, visually polished, integrated gameplay experience over unnecessary architectural complexity.

## 1. PROJECT CONTEXT

Game: AFET UYANIŞI

Engine: Unity 2D / C#

Art style: Handcrafted pixel art

Main gameplay perspective: 2D isometric

Main character: Arda

Controls:
- WASD: Character movement
- E: Interact
- ESC: Close mini-game
- Mouse: Mini-game interaction

Reference resolution: 640×360, 16:9.

The earthquake chapter includes a 120-second preparation countdown, followed by an earthquake sequence.

The player must prepare the house before the earthquake.

One of the preparation tasks is securing a tall wooden bookshelf to the wall.

The player must interact with the bookshelf, enter a cinematic close-up view, complete the anchoring mini-game, and later see the consequences during the earthquake.

The attached storyboard image is the primary reference for the visual design, interaction sequence, feedback, and success/failure states.

IMPORTANT: First inspect the existing Unity project before implementing anything.

Reuse existing:
- Player controller
- Interaction system
- Camera controller
- Timer
- UI architecture
- Scene management
- Art assets
- Audio systems
- Game state management

Do not unnecessarily replace existing working systems.

## 2. PRIMARY DESIGN GOAL

The mini-game must feel like physically securing a bookshelf, not simply answering a multiple-choice question.

The gameplay must include:

1. Approaching the bookshelf.
2. Pressing E.
3. Smooth cinematic camera zoom.
4. Discovering the hazard.
5. Choosing the correct anchoring material.
6. Completing two anchoring interactions.
7. Running a simulated stability test.
8. Receiving visual and audio feedback.
9. Returning to the isometric game.
10. Seeing the consequences during the earthquake.

Target completion time: approximately 12–20 seconds for a successful attempt.

## 3. CAMERA TRANSITION — VERY IMPORTANT

The game normally uses an orthographic isometric camera.

When Arda approaches the bookshelf:

- Display a small floating E interaction prompt.
- Highlight the bookshelf with a subtle outline.
- Pressing E disables normal player movement.
- The camera smoothly moves toward the bookshelf.
- Orthographic size decreases to create a cinematic zoom.
- Transition duration: approximately 0.45–0.65 seconds.
- Use smooth easing.
- Slightly darken the surrounding room.
- Fade in the mini-game interface.

The transition must feel like entering a first-person inspection mode.

DO NOT build a real 3D FPS controller.

Instead, create a 2D close-up interaction interface that simulates a first-person inspection view.

Preferred implementation:

- Keep the original isometric scene running in the background.
- Zoom the camera toward the bookshelf.
- Overlay a detailed bookshelf inspection panel.
- Use a front-facing or three-quarter-view pixel-art bookshelf sprite.
- Keep the bookshelf centered.
- Use mouse interactions to manipulate anchoring points.

The mini-game should appear to be happening directly on the bookshelf rather than inside a disconnected menu.

If the project already contains a camera tweening system, use it.

Otherwise implement a lightweight coroutine-based camera transition.

Store the camera's original position and orthographic size so they can be restored reliably.

## 4. PHASE ONE — HAZARD DISCOVERY

After the zoom transition:

Display the bookshelf in close-up.

Play a brief animation:
- Bookshelf leans approximately 2–4 degrees.
- Returns toward its original position.
- Books wobble slightly.
- A short wooden creaking sound plays.
- Two upper anchoring locations become visible.

Display Arda's dialogue:

"Bu kitaplık duvara sabitlenmemiş! Depremde devrilebilir."

The bookshelf must not actually fall during this inspection.

Show a small prompt:

"Kitaplığı güvenli şekilde sabitle."

Duration: approximately 2 seconds.

Allow the player to continue immediately if they click.

## 5. PHASE TWO — MATERIAL SELECTION

Display three selectable material cards.

OPTION A — CORRECT:
Suitable wall anchors and anti-tip furniture straps.

OPTION B — INCORRECT:
Ordinary shelf screws without proper wall anchoring.

OPTION C — INCORRECT:
Decorative adhesive tape.

Each card must include:
- Pixel-art item icon
- Turkish name
- Hover animation
- Selection highlight
- Click sound

Do not display correct-answer checkmarks before the player makes a choice.

After selection, visually equip the chosen material.

Do not immediately reveal whether the selected material is correct.

The final simulated stability test will reveal the result.

For real-world accuracy, proper anchoring depends on the wall material, furniture design, and suitable hardware. The game must not imply that a generic screw or adhesive tape is sufficient.

Arda is a child/teenage character. Show that an adult assists with the actual installation. The player's interaction represents choosing and coordinating safe preparation, not encouraging a child to drill into walls alone.

## 6. PHASE THREE — INTERACTIVE ANCHORING

The player must complete two anchoring points located near the upper rear corners of the bookshelf.

Display two circular interactive hotspots:

LEFT ANCHOR
RIGHT ANCHOR

Each hotspot starts with:
- Dashed outline
- Small animated pulse
- Unfinished indicator

When the player clicks a hotspot:

Open a short anchoring interaction.

The player must hold the left mouse button while keeping a moving indicator inside a highlighted target zone.

The indicator should simulate the controlled installation of the furniture restraint.

Use:
- Moving progress indicator
- Hold-to-complete interaction
- Small metallic click sounds
- Brief tool animation
- Pixel particles when completed
- A visible strap/bracket appearing on the bookshelf

The player should complete one anchor at a time.

Each anchor takes approximately 2–3 seconds.

If the player releases early:
- Preserve reasonable progress or allow a quick retry.
- Do not punish excessively.
- Do not close the mini-game.

When completed:
- Change hotspot to green.
- Play a satisfying click.
- Show "1/2" or "2/2" progress.
- Display the completed anchor visually.

The two connections must be visually distinct.

IMPORTANT:
This is a game abstraction, not a real installation tutorial. Avoid depicting unsafe or misleading construction instructions.

## 7. PHASE FOUR — STABILITY TEST

After both anchoring points are completed:

Show a large button:

"SAĞLAMLIĞI TEST ET"

When clicked:

- Hide material cards.
- Focus the camera on the bookshelf.
- Play a 2-second simulated horizontal shaking animation.
- Books wobble.
- Camera applies a very subtle impulse.
- Play wooden movement and light rattling sounds.
- Evaluate the selected material and completed anchors.

The shaking is a virtual test inside the game. Do not instruct players to physically shake unsecured furniture in real life.

Implement three outcomes.

### OUTCOME A — SUCCESS

Conditions:
- Correct anchoring material selected.
- Both anchor interactions completed.

Visual result:
- Bookshelf remains upright.
- Straps/brackets visibly hold.
- Small green success particles appear.
- Success icon animates.
- A satisfying confirmation sound plays.

Text:

"Başarılı! Kitaplık güvenli şekilde sabitlendi."

Set:

bookshelfSecured = true

### OUTCOME B — INCOMPLETE ANCHORING

Conditions:
- One or more anchoring points incomplete.

Visual result:
- Bookshelf tilts slightly.
- Yellow warning indicator.
- Incomplete connections pulse.

Text:

"Bağlantılar eksik. Sabitlemeyi tamamla."

Allow the player to continue the installation.

### OUTCOME C — INCORRECT MATERIAL

Conditions:
- Incorrect anchoring material selected.

Visual result:
- Bookshelf tilts noticeably in the virtual test.
- Red warning pulse.
- Short failure sound.

Text:

"Seçilen malzeme uygun değil. Doğru sabitleme yöntemini seç."

Allow the player to return to material selection and retry.

Do not permanently fail the task after a single mistake.

## 8. EXIT TRANSITION

After successful completion:

- Show success feedback for approximately 1 second.
- Fade out mini-game UI.
- Restore the original orthographic camera position and size.
- Smoothly zoom back to the isometric room.
- Re-enable player movement.
- Remove the bookshelf interaction prompt.
- Update the earthquake preparation task list.

Task text:

"✓ Kitaplığı sabitle"

Ensure that the task cannot be completed repeatedly.

If the player presses ESC before completing the mini-game:
- Close the UI.
- Restore camera.
- Restore player controls.
- Preserve appropriate partial progress if the existing architecture supports it.
- Keep the bookshelf task incomplete.

## 9. EARTHQUAKE CONSEQUENCE SYSTEM

The bookshelf must remember whether it was secured.

During the earthquake event, evaluate the saved bookshelf state.

IF bookshelfSecured == true:
- Play bookshelf shaking animation.
- Books wobble slightly.
- The bookshelf remains upright.
- The wall restraints remain visible.

IF bookshelfSecured == false:
- Play a predefined bookshelf tipping animation.
- Books fall to the floor.
- Play falling-object sound effects.
- Spawn a few lightweight pixel-art dust particles.
- Show the resulting environmental hazard.

Use predefined animations rather than expensive realistic physics.

Do not make the player run toward the bookshelf during the earthquake.

During shaking, the player's priority is Drop, Cover, and Hold On.

## 10. COUNTDOWN INTEGRATION

The earthquake chapter contains a 120-second preparation timer.

The mini-game must integrate with the existing timer.

Default behavior:
- Preparation countdown continues while the mini-game is active.
- Camera transition and mini-game must not reset the timer.
- The timer must remain visible in a small corner HUD.
- If the timer reaches zero, safely interrupt the mini-game.
- Close the inspection interface.
- Restore camera and controls as required by the earthquake state.
- Trigger the earthquake sequence exactly once.

No mini-game UI should remain open when the earthquake protection phase begins.

Do not allow anchoring completion after the earthquake has started.

Use the existing authoritative timer and state manager.

## 11. VISUAL DESIGN

Match the supplied reference image.

Visual direction:
- Warm wooden bookshelf
- Dark navy blue UI panels
- Soft orange lighting
- Crisp pixel-art edges
- Pixel-art Arda portrait
- Small polished animations
- Clear Turkish text
- Consistent UI hierarchy

Suggested animation details:
- Smooth camera zoom
- Bookshelf wobble
- Pulsating anchor hotspots
- Material card hover scaling
- Tool click effects
- Installation progress animation
- Small metal sparks or dust particles
- Green success glow
- Red warning pulse
- Camera restoration easing

Keep all animations short and responsive.

Avoid excessive particles, motion blur, or smooth vector effects that conflict with pixel art.

## 12. AUDIO DESIGN

Add audio hooks for:

- Interaction prompt
- Camera zoom
- Wood creak
- Material selection
- Tool installation
- Anchor completion
- Stability test
- Success
- Failure
- Bookshelf tipping during earthquake

Use existing audio clips where available.

If clips are missing, expose AudioClip fields in the Inspector and provide safe fallbacks.

Do not reference missing audio assets.

## 13. SUGGESTED ARCHITECTURE

Create or adapt these components:

BookshelfInteractable.cs
- Detects player interaction.
- Opens the mini-game.
- Prevents repeated completion.

BookshelfMinigameController.cs
- Controls the mini-game phases.
- Handles material selection.
- Handles anchor progress.
- Evaluates stability test.

BookshelfCameraTransition.cs
- Handles smooth camera movement and zoom.
- Saves and restores original camera state.

BookshelfAnchorInteraction.cs
- Handles interactive anchoring hotspots.
- Tracks completion.

BookshelfVisualFeedback.cs
- Plays wobble, tilt, particles, and feedback animations.

BookshelfEarthquakeReaction.cs
- Plays secured or unsecured earthquake animations.

Reuse existing systems where possible.

Do not introduce unnecessary singleton managers or duplicate scene controllers.

Use a simple state machine:

Idle
→ CameraZoom
→ Inspection
→ MaterialSelection
→ Anchoring
→ StabilityTest
→ Success / Retry
→ Exit

Support interruption from any active state when the earthquake countdown expires.

## 14. UNITY IMPLEMENTATION REQUIREMENTS

- Use C#.
- Use the existing Unity version and render pipeline.
- Use Canvas UI for material cards and prompts.
- Use SpriteRenderer or UI Image for close-up visuals.
- Use Animator or lightweight coroutines for transitions.
- Use serialized fields for configurable references.
- Avoid hard-coded scene object names.
- Avoid unnecessary third-party dependencies.
- Preserve existing project functionality.
- Support mouse input and keyboard controls.
- Prevent duplicate interaction events.
- Prevent simultaneous mini-game instances.
- Ensure camera and input states always restore correctly.
- Keep all visual assets replaceable.

Use placeholder sprites only when necessary.

Do not generate fake final artwork or claim placeholder assets are production-ready.

## 15. SCENE INTEGRATION

Integrate this directly into the existing earthquake bedroom scene.

Do not build a disconnected demonstration scene unless needed for testing.

The bookshelf must:
- Exist in the bedroom.
- Have a suitable interaction collider.
- Display the E prompt when Arda is nearby.
- Open the close-up mini-game.
- Save its completion state.
- React correctly during the earthquake.

Use the existing player's interaction range and interaction interface if present.

## 16. ACCEPTANCE TESTS

The implementation is complete only when all these conditions pass:

1. Arda approaches the bookshelf and sees the E prompt.
2. Pressing E opens the mini-game.
3. Camera zooms smoothly toward the bookshelf.
4. Character movement is disabled during interaction.
5. The bookshelf wobble animation plays.
6. Material selection works.
7. Both anchor interactions work independently.
8. Incorrect materials produce failure feedback.
9. Correct materials and both anchors produce success.
10. ESC restores the camera and controls.
11. Successful completion updates the task list.
12. The bookshelf cannot be completed twice.
13. The preparation countdown continues correctly.
14. Countdown expiration safely interrupts the mini-game.
15. The earthquake sequence triggers only once.
16. Secured bookshelf remains upright during earthquake.
17. Unsecured bookshelf plays the tipping animation.
18. No missing-reference or null-reference errors appear in the Console.
19. The complete game remains playable after integration.

## 17. EXECUTION INSTRUCTIONS

Work in this order:

STEP 1:
Inspect the project structure and identify existing gameplay, interaction, timer, camera, and UI systems.

STEP 2:
Briefly report which existing systems can be reused.

STEP 3:
Implement the complete bookshelf mini-game using the project's existing conventions.

STEP 4:
Connect the bookshelf state to the earthquake sequence.

STEP 5:
Connect the completion result to the task list.

STEP 6:
Test all important transitions and interruption cases.

STEP 7:
Report:
- Files created
- Files modified
- Unity scene objects that need configuration
- Inspector references required
- Assets that are still missing
- Tests performed
- Any remaining limitations

IMPORTANT:
Do not stop after writing scripts. Integrate the mechanic into the existing scene and verify as much as the available environment allows.

Do not claim that the mini-game has been fully tested unless you actually executed the relevant tests.

The final result must be a functional, visually coherent, polished mini-game ready to include in AFET UYANIŞI.
~~~

## WP-016 — ROLE

- Kaynak mesaj: 194; satır: 3917; tarih/saat: 10/17/26 12:59:57 PM.
- Tür: Prompt / teknik istek; yapıldığı veya hangi araca gönderildiği tek başına kanıtlanmaz.
- Asıl mesaj gövdesi SHA256: `263f3c525cccb67ebbd543f30e3a794d3cb4612e129bab090df8bf63a3bf850c`.

~~~text
# ROLE

You are a senior Unity 2D gameplay engineer, game designer, UI/UX specialist, and pixel-art animation developer.

Your mission is to implement a complete, polished, interactive *Emergency Backpack Preparation Mini-Game* into our EXISTING Unity project called *AFET UYANIŞI*.

This is a 48-hour game jam project.

The result must be a fully integrated, playable game mechanic — not a standalone prototype, a static UI mockup, or a collection of disconnected scripts.

Prioritize:
1. Playability
2. Visual polish and game feel
3. Integration with existing systems
4. Reliability
5. Performance
6. Simple, maintainable architecture

The attached reference image shows the intended gameplay flow, pixel-art style, UI layout, and animations.

Use it as the primary visual and interaction reference.

---

# 1. EXISTING GAME CONTEXT

Game: AFET UYANIŞI

Engine: Unity 2D

Programming language: C#

Perspective: 2D isometric

Visual style: Warm, handcrafted pixel art

Main character: Arda

Main controls:
- WASD: Move
- E: Interact
- ESC: Exit mini-game
- Mouse: Drag and drop items

Reference resolution: 640×360
Aspect ratio: 16:9

The earthquake chapter includes a 120-second preparation countdown.

Arda must prepare his home before the earthquake begins.

One of his tasks is preparing an emergency backpack.

The player must approach the backpack in the bedroom, press E, enter a cinematic close-up interface, select emergency supplies, physically place them inside the backpack, close it, and return to the main game.

The completed backpack must affect the earthquake chapter's preparation state and aftermath.

IMPORTANT:

Before writing any code, inspect the existing Unity project.

Identify and reuse:
- Player controller
- Interaction system
- Camera controller
- Game manager
- Preparation countdown
- Quest/task manager
- Inventory or item systems, if present
- UI architecture
- Audio system
- Scene management

Do not rebuild systems that already work.

Do not modify unrelated gameplay.

---

# 2. CORE GAMEPLAY EXPERIENCE

The mini-game must follow this sequence:

1. Arda approaches the emergency backpack.
2. An E interaction indicator appears.
3. Player presses E.
4. Camera smoothly zooms toward the backpack.
5. Main player movement becomes disabled.
6. A close-up preparation interface opens.
7. Emergency supplies appear on a table.
8. Player drags supplies into the backpack.
9. Backpack visually fills as items are added.
10. Incorrect items trigger educational feedback.
11. Player closes the backpack using an interactive zipper mechanic.
12. Completion feedback appears.
13. Camera returns to the original isometric view.
14. Preparation task is marked complete.
15. The backpack's completion state is saved for the earthquake aftermath.

Target completion time for a successful attempt: 15–25 seconds.

The mechanic must feel responsive, tactile, and satisfying.

---

# 3. CAMERA TRANSITION

The main game uses an orthographic isometric camera.

When Arda approaches the backpack:

- Show a floating E interaction icon.
- Apply a subtle pixel-art outline to the backpack.
- Display a small Turkish label: "Afet Çantası".
- On pressing E, disable player movement.
- Smoothly move the camera toward the backpack.
- Decrease orthographic size to create a zoom-in effect.
- Transition duration: approximately 0.5 seconds.
- Use smooth easing.
- Slightly darken the room background.
- Fade in the mini-game interface.

IMPORTANT:

Do not create a 3D first-person controller.

Use a 2D close-up inspection view that gives the feeling of interacting directly with the backpack.

The close-up should use:
- A large pixel-art backpack sprite
- An open backpack interior
- A table containing selectable supplies
- A compact item collection panel
- An optional Arda dialogue portrait

Save the original camera position and orthographic size.

When the mini-game ends, restore the original camera state smoothly.

If the existing project uses Cinemachine or another camera system, reuse it.

Otherwise implement a lightweight coroutine-based transition.

---

# 4. PHASE ONE — BACKPACK INSPECTION

After the camera zoom:

Display a large navy-blue emergency backpack on the right side of the screen.

The backpack should initially be closed.

Play a short opening animation:

- Backpack moves slightly forward.
- Zipper opens.
- Front flap moves aside.
- Backpack interior becomes visible.
- A soft fabric and zipper sound plays.

Show Arda's dialogue:

"Afet çantasında neler olmalı? Gerekli eşyaları seçmeliyim."

The dialogue should disappear automatically after approximately 2 seconds or when the player clicks.

The player should immediately understand what to do next.

Display a short instruction:

"Gerekli eşyaları çantaya sürükle."

---

# 5. PHASE TWO — ITEM SELECTION

Create an interactive collection of emergency supplies.

The reference image contains several emergency items and unnecessary objects.

Implement the following item types.

ESSENTIAL SUPPLIES:

1. Drinking water
2. Non-perishable food
3. First aid kit
4. Flashlight
5. Spare batteries
6. Battery-powered or hand-crank radio
7. Copies of identification and important documents
8. Spare clothing
9. Power bank
10. Phone charging cable
11. Whistle
12. Small amount of emergency cash

OPTIONAL / DISTRACTOR ITEMS:

13. Teddy bear
14. Unnecessary decorative object
15. Nonessential bulky entertainment item

Use the reference image for visual guidance.

IMPORTANT EDUCATIONAL RULE:

Do not teach that every optional personal item is inherently wrong.

Some comfort items may be useful, especially for children, provided essential supplies and space requirements are met.

The game should prioritize essential supplies rather than treating every nonessential item as dangerous.

For the game jam, use a simplified item system.

Recommended configuration:
- 9 required item groups
- 3 optional or distractor items
- 9 visible backpack slots

Combine compatible items into item groups when necessary.

For example:
- Flashlight + spare batteries
- Phone cable + power bank
- Water + food can remain separate

Make the required item groups configurable in the Unity Inspector.

Avoid hard-coding the entire item database into UI scripts.

---

# 6. PHASE THREE — DRAG AND DROP

This is the most important gameplay mechanic.

The player must physically drag item icons from the supply table into the open backpack.

Use Unity's EventSystem and appropriate drag interfaces.

Recommended interfaces:
- IBeginDragHandler
- IDragHandler
- IEndDragHandler
- IDropHandler

Each draggable item must support:

- Mouse hover highlight
- Slight scale-up when selected
- Smooth movement following the cursor
- Transparent drag preview
- Small pickup sound
- Drop confirmation sound
- Return animation if dropped outside the backpack

When a valid item enters the backpack:

- Animate the item toward an available slot.
- Play a small bounce.
- Display a brief sparkle or confirmation effect.
- Update the selected-items panel.
- Increase the backpack fill indicator.
- Disable duplicate placement of that item.

The backpack must visibly fill up.

Do not simply increment a counter without showing the object being added.

Use a grid of slots inside the backpack.

Items should remain visually recognizable after being placed.

---

# 7. BACKPACK CAPACITY SYSTEM

Implement a simple 9-slot backpack.

Each required item group occupies one slot.

The player must choose what to pack.

If the backpack becomes full before all required items are collected:

- Show a warning.
- Highlight the missing essential supplies.
- Allow the player to remove an existing item.
- Allow the player to replace unnecessary items.

Text:

"Çanta doldu! Öncelikli ihtiyaçlarını kontrol et."

Do not permanently block the player.

The player should always be able to correct mistakes.

The capacity system must be visually clear.

Avoid complex weight simulation or inventory stacking.

---

# 8. INCORRECT ITEM FEEDBACK

If the player places an unnecessary item in the backpack:

- Allow the item to enter a slot.
- Briefly pulse the slot in amber.
- Show a small educational message.
- Do not immediately fail the entire mini-game.

Example:

"Bu eşya gerekli malzemeler için yer bırakmayabilir."

Display a REMOVE button:

"Çıkar"

When clicked:
- Item returns to the table.
- Slot becomes empty.
- Backpack fill indicator updates.
- A short removal animation plays.

Do not show incorrect-item answers before the player makes a choice.

Avoid repetitive warning popups that interrupt gameplay.

Feedback should be concise and responsive.

---

# 9. PHASE FOUR — INTERACTIVE ZIPPER

Once all required item groups are packed:

Display:

"Çantayı kapat."

The backpack closing action must be interactive.

Do not use a simple button-only interaction.

Implement a zipper drag mechanic.

The zipper starts on the left side of the backpack opening.

The player must:

1. Click and hold the zipper handle.
2. Drag it along a predefined curved or horizontal path.
3. Reach the end of the zipper track.
4. Release the mouse button.

As the player drags:

- The zipper handle follows the cursor along the track.
- The open portion of the backpack gradually closes.
- A zipper sound plays.
- The fabric slightly compresses.
- The zipper progress updates.

If the player releases before reaching the end:
- Preserve progress or smoothly return the zipper to the nearest valid position.
- Allow another attempt.

On successful closure:
- Backpack changes to its closed sprite.
- Play a short bounce animation.
- Show a green confirmation glow.
- Play a satisfying completion sound.

This interaction should take approximately 1–2 seconds.

Use a simple UI slider/path interpolation internally if necessary.

Do not implement expensive cloth physics.

---

# 10. COMPLETION SCREEN

After the zipper closes:

Show a brief success animation.

The backpack should:
- Bounce gently
- Emit small pixel particles
- Display a green checkmark
- Play a positive sound

Show:

"Afet çantası hazır!"

Supporting text:

"Temel ihtiyaçlarını hazırladın."

Display a small progress indicator:

"HAZIRLIK TAMAMLANDI"

Set:

emergencyBagPrepared = true

Update the main task list:

"✓ Afet çantasını hazırla"

Do not mark the task complete until:
- All required item groups are packed.
- The backpack is successfully closed.

---

# 11. EXIT AND RETURN TO MAIN GAME

After successful completion:

- Show success feedback for approximately 1 second.
- Fade out the mini-game UI.
- Restore the original camera position.
- Restore the original orthographic size.
- Re-enable player movement.
- Hide the backpack interaction prompt.
- Update the preparation checklist.

The backpack in the isometric room should now visually appear ready.

Optional:
- Change the backpack sprite from open/unprepared to closed/prepared.
- Add a small checkmark above the backpack.

If ESC is pressed during the mini-game:

- Close the mini-game safely.
- Restore the camera.
- Restore player movement.
- Preserve the selected items if the existing game state system supports it.
- Keep the task incomplete.

The player may return to the backpack and continue.

---

# 12. EARTHQUAKE COUNTDOWN INTEGRATION

The earthquake preparation countdown is 120 seconds.

The mini-game must use the existing countdown.

Rules:

- The countdown continues while the mini-game is open.
- The timer remains visible in a small corner of the screen.
- Opening the backpack must not restart the timer.
- Closing the mini-game must not reset the timer.
- Item interactions must not pause the timer.
- If the countdown reaches zero, the mini-game must be interrupted safely.

On timer expiration:

1. Stop active drag operations.
2. Close the mini-game UI.
3. Cancel zipper interaction.
4. Restore camera ownership.
5. Clear mini-game input locks.
6. Trigger the earthquake sequence exactly once.
7. Prevent further preparation interactions.

IMPORTANT:

Do not allow the mini-game to block the earthquake event.

Use the existing game state manager as the authoritative source of chapter state.

---

# 13. EARTHQUAKE AFTERMATH CONSEQUENCES

The backpack completion state must affect the aftermath.

IF emergencyBagPrepared == true:

- Show the prepared backpack in the aftermath scene.
- Arda can access the prepared supplies after shaking has stopped and it is safe.
- Display a positive preparation result.

IF emergencyBagPrepared == false:

- Show the incomplete or unprepared backpack.
- Display an educational message about missing emergency supplies.
- Reflect the incomplete task in the final preparation score.

Do not force the player to retrieve the backpack during shaking.

During the earthquake, the priority remains:

ÇÖK – KAPAN – TUTUN

---

# 14. VISUAL STYLE AND GAME FEEL

Match the attached reference image.

Visual direction:

- Handcrafted pixel art
- Warm bedroom lighting
- Dark navy UI panels
- Orange highlights
- Crisp pixel edges
- No blurry sprites
- No excessive gradients
- No generic mobile-game appearance

Animation requirements:

- Smooth camera zoom
- Backpack opening
- Item hover bounce
- Item pickup
- Drag ghost preview
- Item snap into slot
- Slot highlight
- Wrong-item pulse
- Item removal
- Backpack fill feedback
- Zipper closing
- Completion bounce
- Green checkmark animation
- Camera zoom-out

Use short animations, generally 0.1–0.5 seconds.

Avoid unnecessary screen shake.

The mini-game should feel polished but remain lightweight.

---

# 15. SOUND DESIGN

Expose configurable audio references for:

- Backpack interaction
- Zipper opening
- Item pickup
- Item drop
- Correct item
- Unnecessary item warning
- Item removal
- Backpack closing
- Completion success

Reuse the project's existing AudioManager if present.

Missing clips must not cause errors.

---

# 16. SUGGESTED CODE ARCHITECTURE

Create or adapt the following components.

EmergencyBagInteractable.cs
- Handles E interaction.
- Opens the mini-game.
- Checks completion state.

EmergencyBagMinigameController.cs
- Controls mini-game states.
- Handles opening, packing, closing, and completion.

EmergencyItemData.cs
- ScriptableObject defining item properties.
- Includes item ID, name, icon, category, required status, and slot cost.

EmergencyItemDragHandler.cs
- Handles draggable supply items.

EmergencyBagDropZone.cs
- Validates item placement.
- Updates slot occupancy.

EmergencyBagInventory.cs
- Tracks packed items.
- Tracks required item groups.
- Prevents duplicates.

EmergencyBagZipperController.cs
- Handles the zipper interaction.

EmergencyBagVisualFeedback.cs
- Handles animations and visual effects.

EmergencyBagEarthquakeState.cs
- Integrates completion state with the aftermath.

IMPORTANT:

These are suggested components, not mandatory files.

If the existing project already has equivalent systems, extend them instead of creating duplicates.

Avoid unnecessary singleton managers.

---

# 17. MINI-GAME STATE MACHINE

Implement a lightweight state machine:

Idle
→ CameraZoom
→ BackpackOpening
→ Packing
→ ReadyToClose
→ ZipperClosing
→ Success
→ CameraReturn

Additional transitions:

Packing → Packing
- When items are added or removed.

ReadyToClose → Packing
- If a required item is removed.

Any active state → Interrupted
- If the earthquake countdown expires.

Any active state → Exit
- If ESC is pressed.

Prevent invalid state transitions.

---

# 18. UI REQUIREMENTS

Use responsive Unity Canvas UI.

Support 16:9 layouts and the project's reference resolution.

Ensure that:
- The backpack is clearly visible.
- Supply items are large enough to drag.
- Item names remain readable.
- UI elements do not overlap.
- Timer remains visible.
- The selected-items panel updates immediately.
- Mouse input works reliably.
- Dragged items remain above other UI elements.
- UI raycasts do not block the intended drop zone.

Use TextMeshPro if already present.

Preserve the project's existing UI scaling conventions.

---

# 19. ACCEPTANCE TESTS

The implementation is complete only when:

1. Arda can approach the backpack.
2. E interaction prompt appears.
3. Pressing E opens the mini-game.
4. Camera zooms smoothly.
5. Player movement becomes disabled.
6. Backpack opening animation plays.

9. Items can be dropped into the backpack.
10. Items snap into available slots.

16. Zipper can be dragged successfully.
17. Success feedback plays.
18. Main task list updates.
19. Camera returns correctly.
20. Player movement is restored.
21. ESC exits safely.
22. Countdown continues during gameplay.
23. Countdown expiration interrupts safely.

25. Backpack state persists into the aftermath.
26. No critical Console errors occur.
27. The complete earthquake chapter remains playable.

Identify relevant scenes, prefabs, scripts, camera systems, timer systems, and UI.
~~~

~~~text
# ROLE

You are a senior Unity 2D gameplay programmer, UI/UX engineer, pixel-art HUD designer, and game feel specialist.

Your task is to implement a complete, polished, event-driven *Earthquake Preparation Quest HUD* into our EXISTING Unity project, AFET UYANIŞI.

The HUD must automatically appear immediately after the earthquake preparation alarm is received and must remain visible in the upper-right corner of the gameplay screen.

IMPORTANT:
This is NOT a standalone quest menu, mockup, or demonstration scene.

Implement the system directly into the existing earthquake bedroom scene.

Use the attached storyboard image as the primary reference for the visual style, task completion animations, progress bar, and quest presentation.

The game is being developed for a 48-hour game jam.

Prioritize reliability, readability, responsiveness, and seamless integration.

---

# 1. GAME CONTEXT

Game: AFET UYANIŞI
Engine: Unity 2D
Language: C#
Art style: Handcrafted pixel art
Perspective: 2D isometric
Reference resolution: 640×360
Aspect ratio: 16:9

Main character: Arda

Controls:
- WASD: Move
- E: Interact
- ESC: Close menus or mini-games

The earthquake chapter contains a 120-second preparation countdown.

The preparation countdown begins after the earthquake simulation alarm is received.

The player must complete preparation tasks before the countdown reaches zero.

The game already contains or is planned to contain:
- Bookshelf anchoring mini-game
- Emergency backpack preparation mini-game
- Earthquake protection mini-game
- Other preparation interactions

The HUD must automatically update when any preparation task is completed.

IMPORTANT SAFETY AND STORY CONTEXT:

The 120-second alarm is a fictional gameplay device, not a realistic prediction of an earthquake two minutes before it occurs.

Present it clearly as a simulated preparation challenge.

Do not imply that real earthquakes can reliably be predicted this way.

---

# 2. CORE EXPERIENCE

Implement the following gameplay sequence:

1. Arda receives the fictional earthquake preparation alarm.
2. Alarm notification appears.
3. A short alarm sound plays.
4. The 120-second preparation countdown begins.
5. The preparation task HUD slides into the upper-right corner.
6. The HUD displays all active preparation tasks.
7. The player explores the bedroom.
8. Completing a mini-game automatically updates the corresponding task.
9. A green checkmark appears beside completed tasks.
10. The progress bar increases smoothly.
11. The HUD provides a short completion animation.
12. The countdown remains visible.
13. When the preparation phase ends, the HUD disappears safely.
14. The earthquake protection phase begins.

The HUD must never require the player to manually mark tasks as complete.

Task completion must be driven by actual gameplay events.

---

# 3. HUD PLACEMENT AND VISUAL DESIGN

Position:
UPPER-RIGHT CORNER.

Unity RectTransform settings:

Anchor Min: (1, 1)
Anchor Max: (1, 1)
Pivot: (1, 1)

Recommended reference-resolution layout:

- Right margin: 8–12 pixels
- Top margin: 8–12 pixels
- Panel width: approximately 175–205 reference pixels
- Height: automatically determined by the number of visible tasks

Use Canvas Scaler:
Scale With Screen Size
Reference Resolution: 640×360
Match Width Or Height: 0.5

Ensure the panel does not cover:
- Arda
- Essential interactions
- Dialogue boxes
- Important mini-game controls

If the existing timer is already in the upper-right corner, integrate it into the quest HUD instead of displaying two overlapping timers.

VISUAL STYLE:

- Dark navy blue background
- Pixel-art panel border
- Slightly lighter blue inner panels
- Cream or off-white pixel text
- Muted orange alarm highlights
- Green completed-task indicators
- Amber active-task indicator
- Gray inactive-task indicators
- Pixelated progress bar
- Minimal, tasteful pixel-art animations

The HUD must match the attached reference image and the existing game's art direction.

Avoid:
- Default Unity UI appearance
- Modern rounded mobile-app cards
- Blurry fonts
- Excessive gradients
- Unnecessary large shadows
- Oversized interface elements

---

# 4. PIXEL FONT — VERY IMPORTANT

Use a real pixel font for the entire quest HUD.

Preferred options:
- Existing pixel font already present in the project
- A properly licensed Turkish-compatible pixel font
- A custom pixel font already used by the game

Do not download or redistribute fonts without checking their licenses.

The font must support Turkish characters:

ç, ğ, ı, İ, ö, ş, ü

Use TextMeshPro if the project already uses it.

Generate or configure the required TMP font asset correctly.

Use:
- Point filtering for pixel-art textures where appropriate
- Sharp glyph rendering
- Consistent font sizing
- Pixel-aligned positioning
- Readable line spacing

Do not artificially shrink text until it becomes unreadable.

At 640×360 reference resolution, aim for approximately:
- HUD title: 10–12 reference pixels
- Task text: 8–10 reference pixels
- Timer: 12–14 reference pixels

Adjust based on the actual font's legibility.

Do not silently replace Turkish letters with English equivalents.

---

# 5. ALARM INTRO ANIMATION

The HUD must not simply appear instantly.

When the alarm event fires:

PHASE A — ALARM

Duration: approximately 0.5 seconds.

- Display a compact warning notification.
- Play a short alarm sound.
- Pulse the warning icon.
- Show text:

"DEPREM HAZIRLIK SİMÜLASYONU"

PHASE B — QUEST HUD REVEAL

Duration: approximately 0.35 seconds.

- Slide the quest HUD in from the right.
- Fade alpha from 0 to 1.
- Animate the header appearing.
- Animate the progress bar appearing.
- Reveal task rows with a short staggered delay.

PHASE C — GAMEPLAY

- HUD remains in the upper-right corner.
- Player retains movement control.
- Countdown runs continuously.
- Quest state updates automatically.

IMPORTANT:

Do not delay or reset the authoritative countdown while the HUD animates.

Do not freeze player movement unnecessarily during the HUD reveal.

---

# 6. HUD STRUCTURE

The HUD should contain:

HEADER:
"AFET HAZIRLIĞI"

TIMER:
"01:59"

PROGRESS:
"0 / 7"

PIXEL PROGRESS BAR

TASK LIST

Use the project's existing quest definitions where available.

Example task data:

TASK 01:
ID: emergency_bag
Text: "Afet çantasını hazırla"

TASK 02:
ID: secure_bookshelf
Text: "Kitaplığı sabitle"

TASK 03:
ID: check_windows
Text: "Pencerelerden uzak güvenli alanı belirle"

TASK 04:
ID: safe_spot
Text: "Çök–Kapan–Tutun alanını öğren"

TASK 05:
ID: assembly_point
Text: "Toplanma noktasını öğren"

TASK 06:
ID: emergency_information
Text: "Acil durum bilgilerini kontrol et"

TASK 07:
ID: family_communication
Text: "Aile iletişim planını öğren"

These are configurable examples.

IMPORTANT:
Inspect the actual project and use the real implemented tasks.

Do not fabricate quest completion logic for tasks that do not exist.

Do not teach unsafe actions such as approaching damaged electrical systems or manipulating gas installations during an earthquake.

---

# 7. TASK ROW STATES

Every task must have three visual states.

INCOMPLETE:
- Gray empty circle
- White text
- No glow

ACTIVE:
- Amber indicator
- Subtle pulse animation
- Slightly brighter text

COMPLETED:
- Green checkmark
- Green text highlight
- Short completion animation
- Optional subtle strikethrough

Each task row must be implemented as a reusable prefab.

Recommended prefab:

QuestHUDRow

Components:
- Icon
- Task text
- Completion indicator
- Optional active-state highlight

Use one shared row component rather than separate scripts for every task.

---

# 8. TASK COMPLETION ANIMATION

When a task is completed:

1. Receive a gameplay completion event.
2. Find the matching task row by task ID.
3. Animate the row briefly.
4. Replace the empty circle with a green checkmark.
5. Pulse the completed row.
6. Play a short confirmation sound.
7. Smoothly update the progress bar.
8. Update the completion counter.
9. Briefly display:

"GÖREV TAMAMLANDI!"

Animation timing:

- Checkmark pop: 0.15 seconds
- Row highlight: 0.3 seconds
- Progress bar fill: 0.35 seconds
- Confirmation message: 0.7 seconds

These timings should be configurable.

IMPORTANT:

The animation must never block player movement or delay the game timer.

If multiple tasks complete at nearly the same time, queue or safely combine visual notifications.

Prevent duplicate task completion animations.

---

# 9. REAL GAMEPLAY INTEGRATION

The quest HUD must be connected to actual gameplay systems.

Example:

When the emergency backpack mini-game succeeds:

QuestManager.CompleteTask("emergency_bag");

When the bookshelf anchoring mini-game succeeds:

QuestManager.CompleteTask("secure_bookshelf");

However, these are conceptual API examples.

Inspect the actual project before deciding which methods to call.

Reuse existing task or quest manager APIs.

If no quest manager exists, implement a lightweight event-driven manager.

Suggested event:

OnQuestCompleted(string questId)

The HUD listens to quest state changes.

The HUD must NOT own the authoritative quest completion state.

The mini-game controller reports completion to the central quest system.

The HUD only visualizes that state.

Ensure task completion is idempotent.

Completing the same task twice must not increase progress twice.

---

# 10. TIMER INTEGRATION

The HUD must display the actual remaining preparation time.

Initial duration:
120 seconds.

Format:
MM:SS

Example:
02:00
01:59
01:58

Use the existing countdown system.

Do not create a second independent countdown.

The timer should update smoothly and accurately.

At 30 seconds remaining:
- Timer text becomes amber.
- A subtle warning pulse begins.

At 10 seconds remaining:
- Timer text becomes red.
- A short warning sound may play once.
- Pulse becomes slightly stronger.

At zero:
- Timer displays 00:00.
- Preparation interactions are disabled.
- HUD closes.
- Earthquake phase starts exactly once.

Do not allow negative time.

Do not allow timer-triggered transitions to occur multiple times.

---

# 11. HUD VISIBILITY RULES

The quest HUD must follow game state.

BEFORE ALARM:
Hidden.

AFTER ALARM:
Visible.

DURING EXPLORATION:
Visible in the upper-right corner.

DURING BOOKSHELF MINI-GAME:
Collapse into a compact version showing only:
- Timer
- Completed task count

DURING BACKPACK MINI-GAME:
Use the same compact version.

DURING DIALOGUE:
Remain visible unless it overlaps important dialogue.

DURING EARTHQUAKE:
Hide the preparation task list.

DURING AFTERMATH:
Optionally show a preparation summary in the results screen.

DURING FLOOD CHAPTER:
Do not show earthquake preparation tasks.

The HUD must never obstruct important interactions.

---

# 12. COMPACT HUD MODE

Implement two display modes.

EXPANDED MODE:
- Title
- Timer
- Progress bar
- Task rows

COMPACT MODE:
- Timer
- Progress counter
- Small progress bar

When a mini-game opens:
- Animate expanded HUD into compact mode.
- Keep the countdown visible.

When the mini-game closes:
- Return to expanded mode if the preparation phase is still active.

Transition duration:
0.2–0.3 seconds.

Avoid resizing jitter or layout flickering.

---

# 13. OPTIONAL PHONE QUEST MENU

If the game already includes a phone UI:

- Keep the phone as an optional detailed quest viewer.
- Allow the player to inspect task descriptions.
- Synchronize the phone quest list with the HUD.
- Do not maintain separate completion states.

The phone and HUD must display the same quest progress.

If the phone UI does not exist, do not build an entirely new phone system just for this feature.

The corner HUD is the primary requirement.

---

# 14. GAME FEEL AND MICRO-ANIMATIONS

Add lightweight polish:

- Alarm icon pulse
- HUD slide-in
- Staggered task reveal
- Active task highlight
- Checkmark pop
- Green completion flash
- Progress bar tween
- Timer warning pulse
- Compact/expanded transitions
- HUD slide-out

Keep all animations short.

Use unscaled time for UI transitions where appropriate, but do not modify the authoritative gameplay countdown.

Avoid excessive camera shake.

Avoid expensive effects.

The HUD must feel like a polished pixel-art game interface.

---

# 15. TECHNICAL ARCHITECTURE

Suggested components:

PreparationQuestHUD.cs
- Controls HUD visibility and animation.
- Subscribes to quest and timer events.

PreparationQuestRow.cs
- Represents a single quest.
- Updates completion and active states.

PreparationQuestManager.cs
- Stores authoritative quest state only if no suitable manager exists.

PreparationHUDAnimator.cs
- Handles lightweight UI transitions.

PreparationTimerDisplay.cs
- Displays the existing countdown.

QuestDefinition.cs
- Optional ScriptableObject for configurable quest data.

Reuse existing equivalents where possible.

Do not introduce unnecessary singleton systems.

Do not create duplicate event subscriptions.

Unsubscribe from events in OnDisable or OnDestroy as appropriate.

Use serialized references rather than repeated FindObjectOfType calls.

---

# 16. UNITY SCENE AND PREFAB SETUP

Integrate the HUD into the existing earthquake scene.

Create a reusable HUD prefab.

Suggested hierarchy:

Canvas
└── PreparationHUD
    ├── Header
    │   ├── AlarmIcon
    │   ├── Title
    │   └── Timer
    ├── Progress
    │   ├── ProgressBarBackground
    │   ├── ProgressBarFill
    │   └── ProgressCounter
    ├── TaskList
    │   └── QuestHUDRow instances
    └── CompletionToast

Use RectTransform anchors correctly.

Use a CanvasGroup for fade animations.

Ensure the HUD respects the existing Canvas and sorting order.

Use 9-sliced pixel-art borders where available.

Avoid adding unnecessary nested canvases.

---

# 17. EDGE CASES

Handle all of the following:

- Alarm triggers twice.
- Quest completion event fires twice.
- Two quests complete simultaneously.
- Mini-game closes unexpectedly.
- Countdown reaches zero during HUD animation.
- Countdown reaches zero during a mini-game.
- Player opens the phone while the HUD is visible.
- Scene reload occurs.
- Earthquake phase begins while a completion animation is playing.
- Quest manager initializes after the HUD.
- A task definition is missing.
- A task has already been completed before the HUD appears.

The HUD must recover gracefully without breaking gameplay.

---

# 18. ACCEPTANCE TESTS

The feature is complete only when:

1. HUD is hidden before the alarm.
2. Alarm activates the preparation HUD.
3. HUD slides into the upper-right corner.
4. Pixel font renders Turkish characters correctly.
5. Timer displays the real preparation countdown.

18. Scene reload does not create duplicate HUD instances.
19. No missing references or critical Console errors occur.
20. The complete earthquake chapter remains playable.

Implement this as a real, working, event-driven quest HUD inside the existing AFET UYANIŞI Unity project.
~~~
